Early in the city's history, millers were required to pay for wheat with cash during the growing season and then to store the wheat until it was needed for flour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELass2000164-343] The [Minneapolis Grain Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Grain_Exchange) was founded in 1881; located near the riverfront, it is the only exchange as of 2023 for [hard red spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat_production_in_the_United_States#Classification_and_uses) wheat [futures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futures_exchange).[^cite_note-344]

Along with cash requirements for the milling industry, the large amounts of capital that lumbering had accumulated stimulated the local banking industry and made Minneapolis a major financial center.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELass2000164,_181-345] The [Federal Reserve Bank of Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_Minneapolis) serves Minnesota, [Montana](19978.md), [North](21651.md) and [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota), and parts of [Wisconsin](33127.md) and [Michigan](18859.md); it has the smallest population of the twelve districts in the [Federal Reserve System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_System), and it has one branch in [Helena, Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena,_Montana).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMisa2013200-346]

Minneapolis area employment is primarily in trade, transportation, utilities, education, health services, and professional and business services. Smaller numbers of residents are employed in government, manufacturing, leisure and hospitality, and financial activities.[^cite_note-347]

In 2024, the Twin Cities metropolitan area had the eighth-highest concentration of major corporate headquarters in the US.[^cite_note-348] Five [Fortune 500 corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500) were headquartered within the city limits of Minneapolis:[^cite_note-Fortune_500-342] [Target Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Corporation), [U.S. Bancorp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Bancorp), [Xcel Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xcel_Energy), [Ameriprise Financial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ameriprise_Financial), and [Thrivent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrivent).[^cite_note-Fortune_500-342] The metro area's [gross domestic product](12594.md) was \$350.7 billion in 2023[^cite_note-BEA-GDP-12] (\$371 billion in 2025).[^cite_note-inflation-US-34]

## Arts and culture

Main article: [Arts in Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_in_Minneapolis)

### Visual arts

White classical building

The [Minneapolis Institute of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Institute_of_Art) admission is free except for special exhibitions.

[^cite_note-349]

During the [Gilded Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilded_Age), the [Walker Art Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Art_Center) began as a private art collection in the home of lumberman [T. B. Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._B._Walker), who extended free admission to the public.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhitmore2004Whitmore_cites_the_1903_''\[\[New_York_Herald\]\]''_which_calls_T._B._Walker_"the_Pine_King_of_the_West"_and_says_"...the_gallery_is_open_to_the_public_six_days_in_the_week,_and_all_who_ring_his_bell_and_ask_to_see_the_old_masters_receive_not_only_permission_from_the_white-aproned_maid_who_answers_the_ring,_but_also_a_catalogue_as_well."-350] Around 1940, the center's focus shifted to modern and contemporary art.[^cite_note-WalkerAbout-351] In partnership with the [Minneapolis Park and Recreation Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Park_and_Recreation_Board), the Walker operates the adjacent [Minneapolis Sculpture Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Sculpture_Garden), which has about forty sculptures on view year-round.[^cite_note-352]

The [Minneapolis Institute of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Institute_of_Art) (Mia) is located in south-central Minneapolis on the 10-acre (4 ha) former homestead of the [Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorilus_Morrison) family.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHess1985\[httpsarchiveorgdetailstheirsplendidleg0000hesspage22mode2up_22\]-353] [McKim, Mead & White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McKim,_Mead_&_White) designed a vast complex meeting the ambitions of the founders for a cultural center with spaces for sculpture, an art school, and orchestra. One-seventh of their design was built and opened in 1915. Additions by other firms from 1928 to 2006 achieved much of the original scheme.[^cite_note-SAH-354] Today the collection of more than 90,000 artworks spans six continents and about 5,000 years.[^cite_note-355]

[Frank Gehry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Gehry) designed [Weisman Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weisman_Art_Museum), which opened in 1993, for the [University of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota).[^cite_note-356] A 2011 addition by Gehry doubled the size of the galleries.[^cite_note-357] [The Museum of Russian Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Russian_Art_\(Minnesota\)) opened in a restored church in 2005, and it hosts a collection of 20th-century Russian art and special events.[^cite_note-358] The [Northeast Minneapolis Arts District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast,_Minneapolis#Arts) hosts 400 independent artists and a center at the [Northrup-King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrup-King) building, and it presents the [Art-A-Whirl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Minneapolis_Arts_Association) open studio tour every May.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360]

### Theater and performing arts

Further information: [List of theaters in Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_theaters_in_Minnesota)

Midnight blue modern building

[The Guthrie Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guthrie_Theater) originated as an alternative to [Broadway](725252.md).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlySchechter197933"In_1963,_the_Tyrone_Guthrie_Theater_was_founded_in_Minneapolis_as_an_alternative_to_Broadway_and_its_commercialism."-361]

Minneapolis has hosted theatrical performances since the end of the American Civil War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlegen1975503-362] Early theaters included [Pence Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pence_Opera_House), the Academy of Music, Grand Opera House, Lyceum, and later the Metropolitan Opera House, which opened in 1894.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlegen1975503–504-363] Fifteen of the fifty-five Twin Cities theater companies counted in 2015 by Peg Guilfoyle had a physical site in Minneapolis. About half the remainder performed in variable spaces throughout the metropolitan area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuilfoyle2015455–484-364]

In his social history of [American regional theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_theater_in_the_United_States), Joseph Zeigler calls the [Guthrie Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guthrie_Theater) the "granddaddy" of regional theater.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZeigler197374,_75,_87,_241-365] [Tyrone Guthrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrone_Guthrie) founded the Guthrie in 1963 with an inventive [thrust stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_stage)—a collaboration by Guthrie, designer [Tanya Moiseiwitsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_Moiseiwitsch), and architect [Ralph Rapson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Rapson)[^cite_note-366]—jutting into the seats and surrounded by the audience on three sides.[^cite_note-Russell-367] French architect [Jean Nouvel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Nouvel) designed a new Guthrie that opened in 2006 overlooking the Mississippi River.[^cite_note-Russell-367] The design team reproduced the thrust stage with some alterations, and they added a [proscenium stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proscenium_stage) and an experimental stage.[^cite_note-Russell-367]

Minneapolis purchased and renovated the [Orpheum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orpheum_Theatre_\(Minneapolis\)), Shubert (now the [Cowles Center for Dance and the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowles_Center_for_Dance_and_the_Performing_Arts)), [State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Theatre_\(Minneapolis,_Minnesota\)), and [Pantages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantages_Theatre_\(Minneapolis\)) theaters, [vaudeville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaudeville) and film houses on [Hennepin Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hennepin_Avenue) that are now used for concerts, plays,[^cite_note-368] and performing arts.[^cite_note-369] Every August, the [Minnesota Fringe Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Fringe_Festival) hosts performances in venues across town.[^cite_note-370] The [May Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_the_Heart_of_the_Beast_Puppet_and_Mask_Theatre#May_Day_Parade_and_Tree_of_Life_Ceremony) is held in south Minneapolis each May.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuftinec20193–4-371][^cite_note-mayday_axios-372]

### Music

Further information: [Music of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Minnesota)

Prince playing guitar at night

[Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_\(musician\)) studied at the [Minnesota Dance Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Dance_Theatre)

[^cite_note-373]

&#x20;through the [Minneapolis Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Public_Schools).

[^cite_note-374]

[Minnesota Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Orchestra) plays classical and popular music at [Orchestra Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchestra_Hall_\(Minneapolis\)) under music director [Thomas Søndergård](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_S%C3%B8nderg%C3%A5rd).[^cite_note-375] The orchestra won a 2014 [Grammy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/56th_Annual_Grammy_Awards#Classical) for their recording of Sibelius's first and fourth symphonies[^cite_note-376] and a 2004 [Grammy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/46th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) for composer [Dominick Argento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominick_Argento) with their recording of *[Casa Guidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_Guidi_\(album\))*.[^cite_note-377] Minneapolis's opera companies include [Minnesota Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Opera),[^cite_note-CBSMNopera-378][^cite_note-379] the Gilbert & Sullivan Very Light Opera Company,[^cite_note-380] and [Really Spicy Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Really_Spicy_Opera).[^cite_note-381]

Singer and multi-instrumentalist [Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_\(musician\)) was a [child prodigy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_prodigy)[^cite_note-CityMusicHistory-382] who was born in Minneapolis and lived in the area for most of his life.[^cite_note-383] In an era of [music scenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indie_music_scene),[^cite_note-384] 1980s Minneapolis was a hotbed for American underground rock alongside R\&B, funk, and soul[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorriganSigelman2018x"At_the_dawn_of_the_1980s,_the_Twin_Cities_music_scene_was_poised_to_explode...._Husker_Du,_the_Replacements,_Loud_Fast_Rules_(later_Soul_Asylum)_and_others_were_fast_building_rabid_local_followings_and_would_soon_achieve_national_acclaim...._At_the_same_time,_a_vibrant_R\&B,_funk,_and_soul_scene_was_maturing_and_forming_what_would_be_known_as_the_"Minneapolis_sound"._The_young_guitar_and_songwriting_virtuoso_Prince_was_perfecting_his_innovative_and_infectious_style..."-385] thanks to the nightclub [First Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Avenue_\(nightclub\)) and musicians like [Hüsker Dü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCsker_D%C3%BC), [The Replacements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Replacements_\(band\)), and Prince.[^cite_note-386] The city hosts several other concert venues including the [Cedar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cedar_Cultural_Center) and the [Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakota_Jazz_Club).[^cite_note-Moran-387] The [Armory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Armory), the [Skyway Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyway_Theatre),[^cite_note-388] and the [Uptown Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_Theater_\(Minneapolis\)) have national management.[^cite_note-389]

### Historical museums

The phrase "Black Lives Matter" painted on a road.

[Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) mural (2020) organized by the [Minnesota African American Heritage Museum and Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_African_American_Heritage_Museum_and_Gallery)

[^cite_note-Eler-390]

Exhibits at [Mill City Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill_City_Museum) feature the city's history of flour milling.[^cite_note-391] [The Bakken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bakken), formerly known as the Bakken Library and Museum of Electricity in Life,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVollmar2003-392] shifted focus in 2016 from electricity and magnetism to invention and innovation, and in 2020 opened a new entrance on [Bde Maka Ska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bde_Maka_Ska).[^cite_note-393] [Hennepin History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hennepin_History_Museum) is housed in a former mansion.[^cite_note-394] Built of elaborate woodwork in 1875 and maintained today as a historic site, the little [Minnehaha Depot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnehaha_Depot) was a stop on one of the first railroads built out of Minneapolis.[^cite_note-395]

The [American Swedish Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Swedish_Institute) occupies a former mansion on Park Avenue.[^cite_note-396] The [American Indian Cultural Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_Cultural_Corridor?action=edit\&redlink=1), about eight blocks on Franklin Avenue, houses All My Relatives Gallery.[^cite_note-397] In 2013, the [Somali Museum of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_Museum_of_Minnesota) opened on Lake Street.[^cite_note-398] The [Minnesota African American Heritage Museum and Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_African_American_Heritage_Museum_and_Gallery) was founded in 2018.[^cite_note-399]

### Libraries and literary arts

Main article: [Hennepin County Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hennepin_County_Library)

In 2008, the [Minneapolis Public Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Public_Library) merged with the [Hennepin County Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hennepin_County_Library). Fifteen of the system's [forty-one branches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hennepin_County_Library_branches) serve Minneapolis.[^cite_note-400] The downtown [Central Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Central_Library), designed by [César Pelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Pelli), opened in 2006.[^cite_note-401] Seven special collections hold resources for researchers.[^cite_note-402]

The nonprofit literary presses [Coffee House Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_House_Press), [Graywolf Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graywolf_Press), and [Milkweed Editions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milkweed_Editions) are based in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-403] The [University of Minnesota Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota_Press) publishes books, journals, and the [Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Multiphasic_Personality_Inventory).[^cite_note-404] The Open Book facility houses [The Loft Literary Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Loft_Literary_Center), Milkweed, and the [Minnesota Center for Book Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Center_for_Book_Arts).[^cite_note-405] Other Minneapolis publishers are [1517 Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1517_Media),[^cite_note-406] [Button Poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Button_Poetry),[^cite_note-407] and [Lerner Publishing Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lerner_Publishing_Group).[^cite_note-408]

### Cuisine

Further information: [Cuisine of the Midwestern United States § Minneapolis and Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_the_Midwestern_United_States#Minneapolis_and_Saint_Paul)

After the flight to the suburbs began in the 1950s, [streetcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcar) service ended citywide.[^cite_note-Wood-409] One of the largest urban [food deserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_desert) in the US developed on the north side of Minneapolis, where as of mid-2017, 70,000 people had access to only two grocery stores.[^cite_note-410] When [Aldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldi) closed in 2023, the area again became a food desert with two full-service grocers.[^cite_note-411] The nonprofit Appetite for Change sought to improve the diet of residents, competing against an influx of fast-food stores,[^cite_note-412] and by 2017 it administered ten gardens, sold produce in the mid-year months at West Broadway Farmers Market, supplied its restaurants, and gave away boxes of fresh produce.[^cite_note-Phillips-413] Appetite for Change closed its Minneapolis restaurant in 2023, opened a food truck, and received a grant from the Minnesota legislature to create a long-term home.[^cite_note-414] West Broadway is one of twenty farmers markets and mini-markets operating in the city, and among them, four are open during winter.[^cite_note-415]

Minneapolis-based individuals who have won the food industry [James Beard Foundation Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Beard_Foundation_Award) include chef [Gavin Kaysen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Kaysen),[^cite_note-416] writer [Dara Moskowitz Grumdahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dara_Moskowitz_Grumdahl),[^cite_note-417] television personality [Andrew Zimmern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Zimmern),[^cite_note-418] and chef [Sean Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Sherman),[^cite_note-419] whose restaurant [Owamni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owamni) received James Beard's 2022 best new restaurant award.[^cite_note-420] James Beard's best new restaurant of 2025 was Bûcheron.[^cite_note-421]

Conceived in Minneapolis as a malted milkshake in candy form, the [Milky Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milky_Way_\(chocolate_bar\)) bar of [nougat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nougat), caramel, and chocolate was made in the [North Loop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Loop,_Minneapolis) neighborhood during the 1920s.[^cite_note-422] Both purported originators of the [Jucy Lucy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jucy_Lucy) burger—the [5-8 Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-8_Club) and [Matt's Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt's_Bar)—have served it since the 1950s.[^cite_note-423] [East African cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_African_cuisine) arrived in Minneapolis with the wave of migrants from Somalia that started in the 1990s.[^cite_note-424] The Herbivorous Butcher, described by CBS News as the "first vegan 'butcher' shop in the United States", opened in 2016.[^cite_note-425]

## Sports

Main articles: [Sports in Minneapolis–Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Minneapolis%E2%80%93Saint_Paul) and [Sports in Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Minnesota)

Remodeled bronze exterior in October 2018. White dog with red target around eye above signage that says Target Center

[Target Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Center)

Nighttime shot of a green ball field and large scoreboard and flood lights lit up behind it

[Target Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Field)

Night aerial shot of large angular modern building lit from within

[U.S. Bank Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Bank_Stadium)

Minneapolis has four professional sports teams. The American football team [Minnesota Vikings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Vikings) and the baseball team [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins) have played in the state since 1961. The Vikings were a [National Football League](21211.md) [expansion team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_team), and the Twins were formed when the [Washington Senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Senators_\(1901%E2%80%931960\)) relocated to Minnesota.[^cite_note-426] The Twins won the [World Series](23489161.md) in 1987 and 1991, and have played at [Target Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Field) since 2010.[^cite_note-427] The Vikings played in the [Super Bowl](27718.md) following the 1969, 1973, 1974, and 1976 seasons, losing all four games.[^cite_note-428]

The basketball team [Minnesota Timberwolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Timberwolves) returned [National Basketball Association](22093.md) (NBA) basketball to Minneapolis in 1989, and were followed by [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) in 1999. Both basketball teams play in the [Target Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Center).[^cite_note-429] The Lynx were the most-successful Minnesota professional sports team and a dominant force in the [Women's National Basketball Association](57721.md) (WNBA), losing the 2024 finals[^cite_note-430] and winning four WNBA championships from 2011 to 2017.[^cite_note-431]

[Minnesota Frost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Frost), the champion [Professional Women's Hockey League](74699992.md) team in 2024 and 2025,[^cite_note-432] and the [Minnesota Wild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Wild), a [National Hockey League](21809.md) team, play at the [Grand Casino Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Casino_Arena),[^cite_note-433] and the [Major League Soccer](71802.md) soccer team [Minnesota United FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_United_FC) play at [Allianz Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allianz_Field). Both venues are located in Saint Paul.[^cite_note-434]

In addition to professional sports teams, Minneapolis hosts a majority of the [Minnesota Golden Gophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers)' [college sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_sports) teams of the University of Minnesota. As of 2026, the [Minnesota Golden Gophers Spirit Squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_Spirit_Squads) have won 30 combined [cheer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Cheerleaders_Association) and [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Dance_Association) national championships.[^cite_note-435] The [Gophers football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_football) plays at [Huntington Bank Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntington_Bank_Stadium) and has won seven [national championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football_national_championships_in_NCAA_Division_I_FBS).[^cite_note-436] The [Gophers women's ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_women's_ice_hockey) team is a six-time [NCAA champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Collegiate_women's_ice_hockey_championship).[^cite_note-437] The [Gophers men's ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_men's_ice_hockey) team plays at [3M Arena at Mariucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3M_Arena_at_Mariucci), and won five [NCAA championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Men's_Ice_Hockey_Championship).[^cite_note-438] Both the [Golden Gophers men's basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_men's_basketball) and [women's basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Golden_Gophers_women's_basketball) teams play at [Williams Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williams_Arena).[^cite_note-439]

The 1,700,000-square-foot (160,000 m<sup>2</sup>) [U.S. Bank Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Bank_Stadium) was built for the Vikings at a cost of \$1.122 billion (\$1.57 billion in 2025);[^cite_note-inflation-US-34] of this, the state of Minnesota provided \$348 million (\$488 million in 2025),[^cite_note-inflation-US-34] and the city of Minneapolis spent \$150 million (\$210 million in 2025).[^cite_note-inflation-US-34] The stadium, which [MPR News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MPR_News) called "Minnesota's biggest-ever public works project", opened in 2016 with 66,000 seats, which was expanded to 70,000 for the [2018 Super Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Super_Bowl).[^cite_note-Nelson-440] U.S. Bank Stadium also hosts indoor running and rollerblading nights.[^cite_note-441] Minneapolis has two municipal [golf courses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golf_course)[^cite_note-442] and one private course.[^cite_note-443] Each January, the [U.S. Pond Hockey Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Pond_Hockey_Championships) are held on [Lake Nokomis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Nokomis).[^cite_note-444] The final weekend of the 2024 championships was canceled due to above average temperatures.[^cite_note-445] A [Boston Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Marathon) qualifier,[^cite_note-446] the [Twin Cities Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twin_Cities_Marathon) moved from early- to mid-October because of rising temperatures.[^cite_note-447]

## Parks and recreation

Main article: [Minneapolis Park and Recreation Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Park_and_Recreation_Board)

[Minnehaha Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnehaha_Park_\(Minneapolis\)) in the summer

Landscape architect [Horace Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_Cleveland)'s masterpiece is the Minneapolis park system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENadenicekNeckar2002\[httpsarchiveorgdetailslandscapearchite00hwscpagen43mode2upqxxxix_xxxix\]"With_other_societal_superintendents_influenced_by_the_ideals_of_New_England,_Cleveland_was_later_able_to_design_and_implement_his_crowning_achievement,_the_Minneapolis_Park_System."-448] In the 1880s, he preserved geographical landmarks and linked them with boulevards and parkways.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENadenicekNeckar2002\[httpsarchiveorgdetailslandscapearchite00hwscpagen45mode2upqxli_xli\]"Cleveland_successfully_linked_boulevards,_small_neighborhood_parks_of_Parisian_derivation,_prairie_ponds_with_wild_islands,_and_lake-edge_parkways"-449] In their introduction to a modern reprint of Cleveland's treatise on [landscape architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landscape_architecture), professors Daniel Nadenicek and Lance Neckar add that "Cleveland was successful in Minneapolis in great measure because he operated with kindred spirits" like [William Watts Folwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Watts_Folwell) and [Charles M. Loring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_M._Loring).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENadenicekNeckar2002\[httpsarchiveorgdetailslandscapearchite00hwscpagen45mode2upqxli_xli\]-450] In his book *The American City: What Works, What Doesn't*, [Alexander Garvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Garvin) wrote Minneapolis built "the best-located, best-financed, best-designed, and best-maintained public open space in America".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarvin201375-451]

Cleveland lobbied for a park on the riverfront to include the city's other waterfall.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200844–46-452] In 1889, [George A. Brackett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_A._Brackett) arranged financing, and his associate Henry Brown paid the state to cover the condemnation of surrounding land.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith200846-453] [Minnehaha Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnehaha_Park_\(Minneapolis\)), containing the 53-foot (16 m) waterfall [Minnehaha Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnehaha_Falls), is one of Minnesota's first state parks.[^cite_note-MRP-454] The falls became what historian Mary Lethert Wingerd calls a "civic emblem" that appears on products and in placenames.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010352–353-455]

The city's parks are governed and operated by the independent [Minneapolis Park and Recreation Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Park_and_Recreation_Board) [park district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_district).[^cite_note-ParkCharter-456] Beyond its network of 185 neighborhood parks,[^cite_note-457] the park board owns the city's street trees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2008xi"The_public_spirit_of_those_who_envisioned_the_future_also_made_sure_Minneapolis_was_a_city_of_trees._Often_lost_in_consideration_of_the_city's_parks_is_that,_from_the_very_early_days_of_the_Minneapolis_park_board,_it_has_been_responsible_for_planting_and_maintaining_street_trees"-458][^cite_note-460] The board owns nearly all land that borders the city's waterfronts—thus the public owns the city's lakeshore property.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2008x"The_first_thing_that_most_visitors_notice_about_Minneapolis_parks_is_one_of_the_unique_features_of_the_city:_nearly_every_foot_of_land_that_borders_water,_other_than_stretches_of_the_Mississippi_River_banks_north_of_Broadway,_is_owned_by_the_park_board."_and_"This_extraordinary_fact_of_public_life_in_Minneapolis,_that_the_people_own_the_waterways..."-461] The park board owns land outside the city limits including its largest park, [Theodore Wirth Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Wirth_Park)—sitting west of downtown Minneapolis and partly in Golden Valley—which incorporates the Eloise Butler Wildflower Garden and Bird Sanctuary.[^cite_note-462]

Group paddling a canoe

Canoeing on the Mississippi

As of 2020, approximately 15 percent of land in Minneapolis is parks, in accordance with the national median, and 98 percent of residents live within one-half mile (0.8 km) of a park.[^cite_note-TPL-463] The city's [Chain of Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_of_Lakes_\(Minneapolis\)) extends through five lakes in southwest Minneapolis.[^cite_note-chain-464] The chain is connected by bicycle, running, and walking paths and is used for swimming, fishing, picnics, boating, ice skating, and other activities. A parkway for cars, a [bikeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segregated_cycle_facilities) for riders, and a walkway for pedestrians[^cite_note-465] run parallel along the 51-mile (82 km) route of the [Grand Rounds National Scenic Byway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Rounds_National_Scenic_Byway).[^cite_note-466]

Parks are interlinked in many places, and the [Mississippi National River and Recreation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_National_River_and_Recreation_Area) connects regional parks and visitor centers.[^cite_note-467] Among walks and hikes running along the Mississippi River, the five-mile (8 km), hiking-only [Winchell Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winchell_Trail) offers views of and access to the [Mississippi Gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Gorge_Regional_Park) and a rustic hiking experience.[^cite_note-468] The [Minneapolis Aquatennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Aquatennial), a civic celebration of the "City of Lakes", is held each July.[^cite_note-469]

Minneapolis's climate provides opportunities for winter activities such as [ice fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_fishing), [snowshoeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowshoeing), [ice skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_skating), [cross-country skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-country_skiing), and [sledding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sledding) at many parks and lakes.[^cite_note-Park_Board_2021-470] As of 2024, the park board maintained 43 outdoor [ice rinks](979488.md) at 20 sites in winter.[^cite_note-471]

## Government

Main articles: [Minneapolis City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_City_Council), [Government of Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Minneapolis), and [Minneapolis Police Department](13087466.md)

Further information: [List of mayors of Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Minneapolis) and [Timeline of race relations and policing in Minneapolis–Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_race_relations_and_policing_in_Minneapolis%E2%80%93Saint_Paul)

Facade of Minneapolis City Hall

Built between 1889 and 1906, [Minneapolis City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_City_Hall), seen from [The People's Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupy_Minneapolis#The_People's_Plaza), is on the [National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillett200741-472]

Through [its municipal branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_DFL), the [Minnesota Democratic–Farmer–Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Democratic%E2%80%93Farmer%E2%80%93Labor_Party) (DFL), affiliated with the national [Democratic Party](5043544.md), is the dominant political force in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-473] The city has not elected a [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Republican_Party) mayor since 1975.[^cite_note-474] At the federal level, Minneapolis is in [Minnesota's 5th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota's_5th_congressional_district), which has been represented by Democrat [Ilhan Omar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilhan_Omar) since 2018. Both of Minnesota's US senators, [Amy Klobuchar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Klobuchar) and [Tina Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Smith), are Democrats who were elected or appointed while residing in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476] [Jacob Frey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Frey), a former city council member,[^cite_note-477] was elected as the [mayor of Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Minneapolis) in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Minneapolis_mayoral_election) and re-elected in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Minneapolis_mayoral_election) and [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Minneapolis_mayoral_election).[^cite_note-478] The city conducts its municipal elections using [instant-runoff voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instant-runoff_voting), which was first implemented ahead of the [2009 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Minneapolis_municipal_election).[^cite_note-Regan-479]

The [Minneapolis City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_City_Council) has 13 members who represent the city's 13 wards.[^cite_note-480] In 2021, a [ballot question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Minneapolis_municipal_election#Question_1) shifted more weight from the city council to the mayor; proponents had tried to achieve this change since the early 20th century.[^cite_note-nathanson-481] The mayor and city council now share responsibility for the city's finances.[^cite_note-McLaughlin-482] The city's primary source of funding is property tax.[^cite_note-483] A sales tax of 9.03 percent[^cite_note-484] on purchases made within the city is a combination of the city sales tax of 0.50 percent, along with county, state, and special district taxes.[^cite_note-485][^cite_note-486] The [Park and Recreation Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Park_and_Recreation_Board) is an independent city department with nine elected commissioners who levy their own taxes, subject to city charter limits.[^cite_note-ParkCharter-456] The Board of Estimation and Taxation, which oversees city levies, is also an independent department.[^cite_note-487]

The mayoral reform ballot measure led to four direct reports to the mayor—two officers, the city attorney, and the chief of staff—and the creation of two new offices.[^cite_note-structure-488] The Office of Public Service is led by the city operations officer. The Minneapolis departments of civil rights and public works report to the office which oversees communications and engagement; development, health, and livability; and internal operations. The Office of Community Safety has a single commissioner responsible for overseeing the police and fire departments, 911 dispatch, emergency management, and violence prevention;[^cite_note-Ibrahim-489] within this office, four emergency response units serve the city: [Behavioral Crisis Response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behavioral_Crisis_Response) (BCR), fire, emergency medical services, and police.[^cite_note-bcr-490] Canopy Mental Health & Consulting, also known as Canopy Roots, operates BCR free of charge[^cite_note-bcr-490] to respond to crises and some 911 calls that do not require police.[^cite_note-491]

A half-dozen officers guarding police station

Police guard the third precinct the day before it was burned down during the [George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests).

After the [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) in 2020, about 166 police officers left of their own accord either to retirement or to temporary leave—many with [PTSD](82974.md)[^cite_note-492]—and a crime wave resulted in more than 500 shootings.[^cite_note-493] A [Reuters](18998750.md) investigation found that killings surged when a "hands-off" attitude resulted in fewer officer-initiated encounters.[^cite_note-494] After Floyd's murder, chiefs reprimanded a dozen officers for misconduct,[^cite_note-495] and as of early 2024, the city had paid out \$50 million for police conduct claims.[^cite_note-496] In 2024 came approval of an independent monitor of a court-enforceable [consent decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_decree), an agreement negotiated with the [Minnesota Department of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Department_of_Human_Rights) and the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) to compel reformed policing practices.[^cite_note-497] In May 2025, the Trump administration moved to dismiss the consent decree.[^cite_note-498]

Violent crime rose three percent across Minneapolis in July 2022 compared with 2021,[^cite_note-499] and in 2020, it rose 21 percent compared to the average of the previous five years.[^cite_note-JanyFeb2021-500] Violent crime was down for 2022 in every category except assaults. Carjackings, gunshots fired, gunshot wounds, and robberies decreased, and homicides were down 20 percent compared to the previous year.[^cite_note-501]

In 2015, the city council passed a resolution making [fossil fuel divestment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_divestment) city policy,[^cite_note-502] joining 17 cities worldwide in the [Carbon Neutral Cities Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_Neutral_Cities_Alliance). Minneapolis's [climate plan](5042951.md) calls for an 80-percent reduction in [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) by 2050.[^cite_note-503] In 2021, the city council voted unanimously to abolish its required minimum number of parking spaces for new construction.[^cite_note-504] Minneapolis has a separation ordinance that directs local law-enforcement officers not to "take any law enforcement action" for the sole purpose of finding [undocumented immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undocumented_immigrant), nor to ask an individual about their immigration status.[^cite_note-505]

## Education

### Primary and secondary

In 1834, volunteer missionaries [Gideon and Samuel Pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gideon_Hollister_Pond)[^cite_note-506] sought permission for their work from the US Indian agency at Fort Snelling.[^cite_note-507] They taught new farming techniques and their Christian religion to Chief [Cloud Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloud_Man) and his Dakota community on the east shore of Bde Maka Ska.[^cite_note-religion-329] That year, J. D. Stevens and the Ponds built an Indian mission near [Lake Harriet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Harriet_\(Minnesota\)), which was the first educational institution in the Minneapolis area.[^cite_note-religion-329] In the treaty of 1837, the US promised payment to the Dakota, but instead gave the monies to the missionaries earmarked for education, and in protest, fewer than ten Dakota students attended.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClemmons2005181-508] After more settlers moved to the area, ten school buildings served nearly 4,000 students by 1874. The district had more than one hundred schools when enrollment peaked at 90,000 students in 1933.[^cite_note-509]

Man teaching a full classroom

A dual language science outreach at [Emerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Public_Schools#Elementary_schools_\(K%E2%80%935\)), one of nine

[^cite_note-510]

&#x20;magnet elementary schools

[Minneapolis Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Public_Schools) has room for 45,000 students and enrolled about 28,500 [K–12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%E2%80%9312) students as of 2024,[^cite_note-511] in more than fifty schools, divided between community and [magnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnet_school).[^cite_note-Whitler-512] Poor financial oversight of the district resulted in a \$37.9 million deficit—and thus staff layoffs—for the 2025–2026 school year.[^cite_note-513] As of 2023, enrollment was declining about 1.5 percent per year, and approximately 60 percent of school age children attended district schools.[^cite_note-514] The city offered two reasons for the decline: a dwindling number of children lived in the city since 2020 and, accounting for one-fifth of the decline, the climbing popularity of charter schools and open enrollment.[^cite_note-515] Many students enrolled in alternatives such as charter schools, of which the city had 28 as of 2024.[^cite_note-516] By state law, charter schools are open to all students and are tuition-free.[^cite_note-517] In 2022, about 1200 at-risk students attended district alternative schools that offered them better outcomes than traditional schools.[^cite_note-518] For the 2022–2023 school year, 368 students were [homeschooled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling) in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-519]

School district demographics were 41 percent White students, 35 percent Black, 14 percent Hispanic, and 5 percent each were Asian and Native American.[^cite_note-MPS-520] [English-language learners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-language_learner) were about 17 percent[^cite_note-MPS-520] in a district that spoke 100 languages at home.[^cite_note-521] About 15 percent were [special education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_education) students.[^cite_note-MPS-520] As of fall 2023, every public school student in the state receives one free breakfast and one free lunch each school day.[^cite_note-522] In 2025, the district's overall graduation rate was 73 percent, a percent less than the previous year.[^cite_note-523]

### Colleges and universities

See also: [Minnesota State Colleges and Universities system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_State_Colleges_and_Universities_system)

striking geometric metallic building in front of more traditional ones

[University of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota) teaching [art museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weisman_Art_Museum), teaching [hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota_Medical_Center), and [student union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffman_Memorial_Union) (left to right)

Headquartered in Minneapolis, the [University of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Minnesota) Twin Cities campus enrolled more than 45,000 students in 2025–2026.[^cite_note-USNews-524] College rankings in 2025–2026 place the school in the range of 51st[^cite_note-Shanghai-525] to 210th for academics worldwide.[^cite_note-THE-526][^cite_note-QS-527] [QS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings) found a decline in rank over a decade.[^cite_note-QS-527] [Shanghai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities) found excellence in communication, psychology, and library and information science.[^cite_note-Shanghai-525] Among the 2,250 schools *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report)* compared in its 2025-2026 best global universities rankings, the University of Minnesota ranked 72nd.[^cite_note-USNews-524] The school has unusual autonomy that has existed in Minnesota since 1858, when the state constitution included the provision that [regents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regent) are in control, independent of city government.[^cite_note-528] Founded in 1851[^cite_note-QS-527] and closed in its first decade for lack of funding, the University of Minnesota was revived under the [Morrill Act of 1862](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrill_Land-Grant_Acts) using land taken from the Dakota people.[^cite_note-morillgrant-529][^cite_note-532]

[Augsburg University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augsburg_University), [Minneapolis College of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_College_of_Art_and_Design), and [North Central University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Central_University) are private four-year colleges; the first two offer master's programs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Princeton_Review201449,_490,_538-533] The public two-year [Minneapolis Community and Technical College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Community_and_Technical_College)[^cite_note-534] and the private [Dunwoody College of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunwoody_College_of_Technology)[^cite_note-535] provide career training and associate degrees, and the latter offers a bachelor's program. [Saint Mary's University of Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Mary's_University_of_Minnesota) has a Twin Cities campus for its graduate and professional programs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Princeton_Review2014655-536] Opening a new Minneapolis site in 2024, [Red Lake Nation College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lake_Nation_College) is an accredited federally recognized [tribal college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_colleges_and_universities) site that teaches [Ojibwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ojibwe) culture and awards associate degrees.[^cite_note-537] The large, principally [online universities](8997.md) [Capella University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capella_University)[^cite_note-538] and [Walden University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walden_University_\(Minnesota\))[^cite_note-539] are both headquartered in the city. The public four-year [Metropolitan State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_State_University)[^cite_note-540] and the private four-year [University of St. Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_St._Thomas_\(Minnesota\))[^cite_note-541] are post-secondary institutions based elsewhere that have campuses in Minneapolis. The city has more than twenty-five licensed career schools.[^cite_note-542]

## Media

Main article: [Media in Minneapolis–Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Minneapolis%E2%80%93Saint_Paul)

As of March 2024, Minnesota Newspaper Association members who publish in Minneapolis include *Insight News*, *[Finance & Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_&_Commerce)*, *Longfellow Nokomis Messenger*, *[Minneapolis/St. Paul Business Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_City_Business_Journals#List_of_publications)*, *[Minnesota Spokesman-Recorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Spokesman-Recorder)*, *[Minnesota Women's Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Women's_Press)*, *North News*, *Northeaster*, *Southwest Connector*, *[Star Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Tribune)*, and *St. Paul – Midway Como Frogtown Monitor*.[^cite_note-MNA-543] *[La Prensa de Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Prensa_de_Minnesota)*,[^cite_note-544] *[Vida y Sabor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vida_y_Sabor)*,[^cite_note-545] and *[The American Jewish World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Jewish_World)*[^cite_note-546] are published in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECornell2016298-547] Other papers are *Southwest Voices*,[^cite_note-548] Streets.mn,[^cite_note-549] *[Bring Me The News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bring_Me_The_News)*,[^cite_note-550] *[Racket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racket_\(Minnesota\))*,[^cite_note-551] *[MinnPost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MinnPost)*,[^cite_note-552] and *[Minnesota Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Daily)*.[^cite_note-553]

*Media Tales* called Minnesota a "plentiful" source of national [trade magazines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_magazine); companies in Minneapolis publish *Foodservice News* and *[Franchise Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franchise_Times)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKellerO'Meara200786-554] Some other magazines published in the city are *American Craft*;[^cite_note-555] business publications *Enterprise Minnesota*[^cite_note-556] and *Twin Cities Business*;[^cite_note-557] the literary journal *[Rain Taxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_Taxi)*;[^cite_note-558] university student publications *Great River Review*,[^cite_note-559] *[Minnesota Journal of International Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Journal_of_International_Law)*,[^cite_note-560] and *[Minnesota Law Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Law_Review)*;[^cite_note-561] and professional magazines *Architecture Minnesota*,[^cite_note-562] *[Bench & Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_State_Bar_Association#Projects_and_publications)*,[^cite_note-563] and *[Minnesota Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Medical_Association#Publications)*.[^cite_note-564]

In 2023, [Nielsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_Holdings) found the Minneapolis–Saint Paul area to be the 15th-largest [designated market area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_market) which is down from 14th in 2022.[^cite_note-565] Of the 89 FM and 57 AM stations that can be heard in the city, 17 FM stations and 11 AM stations are licensed in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-566] The Twin Cities have 1,742,530 TV homes.[^cite_note-567] *[TV Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Guide)* lists 151 TV channels for Minneapolis.[^cite_note-568]

## Infrastructure

### Transportation

Main articles: [Transportation in Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Minnesota), [Metro (Minnesota)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_\(Minnesota\)), and [Trails in Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trails_in_Minneapolis)

Yellow and blue light rail train at a stop

A Green Line train traveling from the [Stadium Village station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stadium_Village_station)&#x20;

Three agents converse on light rail

[Metro Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Transit_\(Minnesota\)) trip agents on the Blue Line in 2024

For all trips by all members of a household in 2019, [Metropolitan Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Council_\(Minnesota\)) data showed that the most common means of transportation was driving alone (40 percent), the least common was bicycling (3 percent), and others were carpooling (28 percent), walking (16 percent), and public transit (13 percent). The city's goal is that by 2030, 60 percent of trips are taken without a car, or 35 percent by walking and biking and 25 percent by transit. The city aims to reduce vehicle miles traveled by 1.8 percent per year.[^cite_note-569]

A division of the Metropolitan Council, [Metro Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Transit_\(Minnesota\)) operates public transportation in the Minneapolis–Saint Paul metropolitan area.[^cite_note-570] As of 2026, the system has two [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) lines and eight [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit) (BRT) lines.[^cite_note-571][^cite_note-\:0-572] A fleet of 736 buses serves 10,745 bus stops.[^cite_note-transitfacts-573] As of 2021, riders of Metro Transit system-wide were 55 percent persons of color.[^cite_note-transitfacts-573] The system provided nearly 45 million rides in 2023, a sixteen-percent increase over the previous year.[^cite_note-Rantala-574] In 2023, bus service had returned to 90 percent of its ridership before the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-Rantala-574]

The [Metro Blue Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Blue_Line_\(Minnesota\)) light rail line connects the [Mall of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mall_of_America) and [Minneapolis–Saint Paul International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis%E2%80%93Saint_Paul_International_Airport) in [Bloomington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington,_Minnesota) to downtown,[^cite_note-575] and the [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_\(Minnesota\)) travels from downtown through the University of Minnesota campus to downtown [Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Paul,_Minnesota).[^cite_note-576] A [Blue Line extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Blue_Line_Extension_\(Minnesota\)) to the northwest suburbs is scheduled to be built and completed by 2030.[^cite_note-577] A [Green Line extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Green_Line_Extension_\(Minnesota\)) is planned to connect downtown with the southwestern suburbs.[^cite_note-579] BRT lines are 25 percent faster than regular bus lines because riders pay before boarding, stops are limited, and sometimes they employ signal prioritization. Metro Transit plans to convert existing bus lines to BRT in the coming years.[^cite_note-Brey-580][^cite_note-581] In January 2026, bus service replaced the former [Northstar Commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northstar_Line) from [Big Lake, Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Lake,_Minnesota), to downtown Minneapolis.[^cite_note-\:0-572]

Cyclist waiting at a stoplight in the snow.

A cyclist in winter

Hundreds of homeless people nightly sought shelter on Green Line trains until overnight service was cut back in 2019.[^cite_note-582] Short more than a hundred police officers, in 2022, the Metro Council hired community groups to help police light rail stations; these non-profits can guide passengers to mental health services and shelters.[^cite_note-MooreLRT-583] In partnership with a private security company in 2024, Metro Transit improved security and safety with trip agents who ride the light rail lines each day and work with transit police and community officers[^cite_note-584]—as of 2026, 139 trip agents had cited about 0.2 percent of riders for nonpayment or other violations out of 2 million inspections.[^cite_note-585]

In 2007, the [Interstate 35W bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-35W_Mississippi_River_bridge) over the Mississippi, which was overloaded with 300 short tons (270,000 kg) of repair materials, collapsed, killing 13 people and injuring 145. The [bridge was rebuilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-35W_Saint_Anthony_Falls_Bridge) in 14 months.[^cite_note-586]

Evie Carshare, owned by Minneapolis and Saint Paul since 2022, is a fleet of 145 electric cars available for one-way trips in a 35-square-mile (91 km<sup>2</sup>) area of the Twin Cities.[^cite_note-587] In warm weather, [Lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_\(transportation_company\)) and Veo have shared electric bikes and scooters for rent at sixty mobility hubs located on transit lines; riders may end their trip anywhere in the city.[^cite_note-588]

Minneapolis has 16 miles (26 km) of on-street protected bikeways, 98 miles (158 km) of bike lanes, and 101 miles (163 km) of off-street bikeways and trails.[^cite_note-589] Off-street facilities include the [Grand Rounds National Scenic Byway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Rounds_National_Scenic_Byway), [Midtown Greenway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Greenway), [Little Earth Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Earth_Trail), [Hiawatha LRT Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiawatha_LRT_Trail), [Kenilworth Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenilworth_Trail), and [Cedar Lake Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Lake_Trail).[^cite_note-590] The [Minneapolis Skyway System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis_Skyway_System), 9.5 miles (15.3 km) of enclosed pedestrian bridges called [skyways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyway), links 80 city blocks downtown with access to second-floor restaurants, retailers, government, sports facilities, doctor's offices, and other businesses that are open on weekdays.[^cite_note-591] Fifteen commercial passenger airlines serve [Minneapolis–Saint Paul International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis%E2%80%93Saint_Paul_International_Airport) (MSP).[^cite_note-592] MSP is the headquarters of [Sun Country Airlines](1241139.md).[^cite_note-MSP-593] After it merged with [Northwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Airlines) in 2009, [Delta Air Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Air_Lines) flew 80 percent of the airport's traffic,[^cite_note-594] and MSP was Delta's second-largest US hub.[^cite_note-595]

### Services and utilities

Woman in uniform on Marquette Av downtown

A Downtown Improvement District ambassador

[Xcel Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xcel_Energy) supplies electricity,[^cite_note-CEP-596] and [CenterPoint Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CenterPoint_Energy) provides gas.[^cite_note-CEP-596] The water supply is managed by four [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin) districts that correspond with the Mississippi and three streams that are river tributaries.[^cite_note-597]

The city has nineteen [fire stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_station).[^cite_note-598] Requests for non-emergency information or service requests can be made through Minneapolis [311](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3-1-1). The call center operates in English, Spanish, Hmong, and Somali, and offers 220 language options.[^cite_note-599] Email, TTY, text, voice, and a mobile app can access the center.[^cite_note-600]

The Minneapolis department of public works is responsible for services including snow plowing, solid waste removal, traffic and parking, water treatment, transportation planning and maintenance, and fleet services for the city.[^cite_note-601] Among its engineering functions, the department was increasing the capacity of a 4,200-foot (1,300 m) [storm water tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storm_drain) system 80 feet (24 m) under Washington to Chicago avenues and had completed 97 percent of the excavation phase and 41 percent of the lining phase as of August 2023.[^cite_note-602] Designed for downtown's concrete landscape, the system will drain runoff into the Mississippi in case of a [100-year storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100-year_flood).[^cite_note-Vue-603]

Downtown [Improvement District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_improvement_districts_in_the_United_States) ambassadors, who are identified by their blue-and-green-yellow fluorescent jackets, daily patrol a 120-block area of downtown to greet and assist visitors, remove trash, monitor property, and call police when they are needed. The ambassador program is a [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) that is paid for by a special downtown tax district.[^cite_note-604]

### Health care

See also: [COVID-19 pandemic in Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Minnesota) and [COVID-19 pandemic in Minnesota § Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Minnesota#Economy)

Four-story building seen from across the street

[Hennepin County Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hennepin_County_Medical_Center) has the state's busiest emergency room.

[^cite_note-605]

Hennepin County Medical Center, a public [teaching hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teaching_hospital) and [Level I trauma center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Level_I_trauma_center),[^cite_note-HH-606] opened in 1887 as City Hospital.[^cite_note-607] The city is also served by [Abbott Northwestern Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbott_Northwestern_Hospital), [Children's Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Minnesota), and University of Minnesota and veterans medical centers.[^cite_note-608]

[Cardiac surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiac_surgery) was developed at the University of Minnesota's [Variety Club Heart Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M_Health_Fairview_University_of_Minnesota_Masonic_Children's_Hospital#History).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeffrey2001p._59-609] Surgeon [F. John Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._John_Lewis) successfully repaired a child's [congenital heart defect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congenital_heart_defect) in 1952.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoss2005S2210-610] By 1957, more than 200 patients—most of whom were children—had survived open-heart surgery.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeffrey2001p._61-611] Working with surgeon [C. Walton Lillehei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Walton_Lillehei), [Medtronic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medtronic) began to build portable and implantable [cardiac pacemakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiac_pacemaker) about this time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeffrey2001p._65-612]

In 2022, opioid overdoses killed 231 persons in Minneapolis.[^cite_note-cityopioids-613] For the state in 2021, Black persons were three times and Native American persons were ten times more likely to die from an opioid overdose than White persons.[^cite_note-614][^cite_note-616] The 2024 city budget added funds for the Turning Point treatment center, which provides care specifically for African Americans.[^cite_note-budget2004-222] The [Red Lake Band of Chippewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Lake_Band_of_Chippewa) is building a culturally sensitive treatment center for opioid and fentanyl addiction. Minneapolis transferred two city-owned properties to the Red Lake Nation for the facility.[^cite_note-617][^cite_note-618]

The Mashkiki Waakaa'igan Pharmacy—funded by the [Fond du Lac Band of Lake Superior Chippewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fond_du_Lac_Band_of_Lake_Superior_Chippewa)—dispenses free prescription drugs and culturally sensitive care to members of any federally recognized tribes living in Hennepin and Ramsey counties, regardless of insurance status.[^cite_note-KFF-619]

## Sister cities

Minneapolis's [sister cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) are:[^cite_note-620]

-  [Bosaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosaso), Somalia (2014)

-  [Cuernavaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuernavaca), Mexico (2008)

-  [Eldoret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eldoret), Kenya (2000)

-  [Harbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harbin), China (1992)

-  [Ibaraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibaraki,_Osaka), Japan (1980)

-  [Kuopio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuopio), Finland (1972)

-  [Najaf](200512.md), Iraq (2009)

-  [Novosibirsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novosibirsk), Russia (1988)

-  [Santiago](51572.md), Chile (1961)

-  [Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tours), France (1991)

-  [Uppsala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uppsala_Municipality), Sweden (2000)

-  [Winnipeg](100730.md), Canada (1973)

[^cite_note-37]: &#x20;Because President Thomas Jefferson had not authorized Pike's trip, which was made at the behest of [James Wilkinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Wilkinson), the new governor of the Louisiana territory, Pike did not have the authority to make a treaty.<sup>[\[31\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeber202214-33)</sup> Pike valued the land at \$200,000 (\$4.3 million in 2025)<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-inflation-US-34)</sup> in his journal but omitted the value in Article 2 of the treaty. Pike gave the chiefs 60 US gallons (230 L) of liquor and \$200 (\$4,300 in 2025)<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-inflation-US-34)</sup> in gifts at the signing.<sup>[\[33\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012141-35)</sup> In 1808, the US Senate authorized one hundredth of Pike's estimate and added acreage,<sup>[\[33\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012141-35)</sup> paying \$2,000 (\$43,000 in 2025)<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-inflation-US-34)</sup> for the land in 1819.<sup>[\[34\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeber202213-36)</sup>

[^cite_note-55]: &#x20;In the 1851 [Treaty of Traverse des Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Traverse_des_Sioux) and [Treaty of Mendota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Mendota), the US took all Dakota land west of the Mississippi,<sup>[\[41\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELass2000108-44)</sup> about 24 million acres (97,000 km<sup>2</sup>),<sup>[\[42\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012182-45)</sup> in exchange for a 10-mile (16 km) wide reservation on the Minnesota River<sup>[\[43\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFolwell1921\[httpsarchiveorgdetailshistoryofminneso02folwpage216_216]-46)</sup> and about \$3 million (\$116 million in 2025).<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-inflation-US-34)</sup> After expenses, the Dakota were promised fifty years of annuities in goods<sup>[\[44\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012171-47)</sup> and interest on \$1,360,000 (\$52.6 million in 2025) and \$1,410,000 (\$54.6 million in 2025);<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-inflation-US-34)</sup> the US kept the principal.<sup>[\[45\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson201930-48)</sup> The Dakota could not read English, and their interpreters worked for the US.<sup>[\[40\]](#cite_note-MNtreaties-43)</sup> In Mendota, negotiator [Wakute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wacouta_I) said he feared signing a treaty because the prior treaty was changed from the one he had signed.<sup>[\[46\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite20125,_188-49)</sup> The US Congress ratified amendments after the fact, and refused to consider payment unless the Dakota agreed to their new terms—in 1852 Congress struck the reservation from the final treaty.<sup>[\[47\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010197-50)</sup> Negotiators [Luke Lea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Indian_Affairs#Commissioners_and_assistant_secretaries) and [Alexander Ramsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Ramsey) had promised the Dakota they would prosper, and they rushed the transaction.<sup>[\[48\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010189–192-51)</sup> The chiefs were asked to sign a third paper in 1851—onlookers assumed it was a third copy of the treaty<sup>[\[49\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012180–181-52)</sup>—that Ramsey later declared was a "solemn acknowledgment" of the Dakota's debt to traders.<sup>[\[50\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestermanWhite2012191-53)</sup> Ramsey, as territorial governor, enforced the trader's paper, distributing the monies to himself, [Henry Sibley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Hastings_Sibley), and their friends.<sup>[\[51\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson201932–33-54)</sup>

[^cite_note-60]: &#x20;Part of the delay was a month's indecision in the US Treasury about appropriating gold or greenbacks and in Congress, which was preoccupied with Civil War finance. Gold arrived in the region just a few hours after settlers had been killed and war had begun.<sup>[\[55\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFolwell1921237–238-59)</sup>

[^cite_note-71]: &#x20;General<sup>[\[62\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010312-67)</sup> [Henry Sibley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Hastings_Sibley) rushed to complete the trials before winter.<sup>[\[63\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019225-68)</sup> Trials were held from late September<sup>[\[64\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019217-69)</sup> through early November 1862, in central Minnesota west of Minneapolis;<sup>[\[63\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019225-68)</sup> on each day up to forty-three men stood trial.<sup>[\[63\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019225-68)</sup> The Dakota men were without counsel, rarely spoke English, in some cases trials proceeded without witnesses, and no time was made for cross-examination.<sup>[\[65\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019228-70)</sup> Historian Gary Clayton Anderson says, "In 90 percent of the trials, the entire event lasted only a minute or two...".<sup>[\[65\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019228-70)</sup>

[^cite_note-84]: &#x20;Sibley appointed a commission of men thought later to be biased to hear the trials and planned to carry out executions immediately.<sup>[\[66\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010313-72)</sup> Of 400 Dakota, 303 were sentenced to death, 20 were sentenced to prison, 69 were acquitted, and 8 were released.<sup>[\[67\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010314-73)</sup> When his superior Major General [John Pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Pope_\(general\)) reported the commission's findings to President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) he had realized only the president can authorize executions. Historian Mary Lethert Wingerd writes that Lincoln and members of his cabinet were "taken aback" by the number of condemned and the irregular proceedings.<sup>[\[68\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010316-74)</sup> Lincoln then ordered a [stay of execution](3028902.md) until he could review the trial transcripts.<sup>[\[68\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010316-74)</sup> Minnesotans wanted revenge and many were outraged at the stay.<sup>[\[69\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010318-75)</sup> Lincoln was under pressure from Minnesotans,<sup>[\[70\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010319-76)</sup> and wrote that he wished to avoid cruelty and to discourage another outbreak.<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-MNHStrials-77)</sup> He first decided that only rapists would be hanged, but only 2 Dakota met that condition. Then with the help of his lawyers,<sup>[\[72\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019251-78)</sup> Wingerd writes that Lincoln "reluctantly"<sup>[\[70\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010319-76)</sup> ordered that 39 men<sup>[\[73\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019253-79)</sup> would be hanged; these men had been convicted of murdering civilians. One received a last minute reprieve.<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-MNHStrials-77)</sup> Minnesotans participated in lynch mobs and vigilantism against the Dakota, both condemned and friendly—2 men died of injuries sustained during attacks on Sibley's wagon train that took them to [Mankato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mankato). Command transferred to Colonel [Stephen Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Miller_\(Minnesota_governor\)) who oversaw the executions—he declared martial law and banned alcohol for the 4,000 spectators.<sup>[\[74\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010324,_326-80)</sup> The Dakota were reportedly cheerful as they walked to their deaths; a journalist wrote, "No equal number ever approached the gallows with greater courage, and more perfect determination to prove how little death can be feared".<sup>[\[75\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010327-81)</sup> After what was the largest mass execution in US history,<sup>[\[76\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2019262-82)</sup><sup>[\[75\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010327-81)</sup> Minnesota officials discovered that in their haste, they had hanged 2 innocent men.<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-MNHStrials-77)</sup> Nearly all the men's bodies were dug up from their graves within 24 hours, some for trophies but most by physicians who wanted cadavers to dissect.<sup>[\[77\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWingerd2010327,_328-83)</sup>

[^cite_note-94]: &#x20;The University of Minnesota Dakota Dictionary Online requires a Dakota font to read special characters.<sup>[\[85\]](#cite_note-92)</sup> Here, Dakota to Latin alphabet transliteration is borrowed from [Lerner Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lerner_Publishing_Group) in Minneapolis.<sup>[\[86\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKimmererSmith2022302-93)</sup>

[^cite_note-98]: &#x20;In [Atwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Atwater)'s history, Baldwin gives the Sioux word as *Minne*.<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaldwin1893a39-5)</sup> [Riggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Return_Riggs) gives *mini*.<sup>[\[87\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERiggs1992314-95)</sup> [Williamson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Poage_Williamson) who was most familiar with [Santee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakota_people) has *Mini*, and in the [Yankton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakota_people) dialect, *mni*.<sup>[\[88\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamson1992257-96)</sup> Here, *mni* is from the University of Minnesota Dakota Dictionary Online.<sup>[\[89\]](#cite_note-97)</sup>

[^cite_note-104]: &#x20;"Minneapolis would be the nation's flour capital for 50 years." and "Begun in 1848, timber milling had lasted for almost 50 years."<sup>[\[94\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnfinsonMadiganForsbergNunnally2003-103)</sup>

[^cite_note-106]: &#x20;Soldiers from Fort Snelling built a [sawmill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sawmill) in 1820, and a [gristmill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gristmill) in 1823, on the west bank near the falls.<sup>[\[92\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELieblingMorrison196618-101)</sup><sup>[\[93\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKane1987165-102)</sup><sup>[\[j\]](#cite_note-104)</sup> The city's first commercial sawmill was built in 1848, and the first commercial gristmill in 1849.<sup>[\[95\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGras1922300–301-105)</sup>

[^cite_note-130]: &#x20;In 1928, Washburn-Crosby merged with other local millers and changed its name to General Mills to reflect a wider product base including convenience foods like [Wheaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheaties).<sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanbom2003283-129)</sup>

[^cite_note-214]: &#x20;In a 1975 article, reporter John Carman said the city's highest point is 967 feet (295 m) at Deming Heights Park in the [Waite Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waite_Park,_Minneapolis) neighborhood.<sup>[\[198\]](#cite_note-212)</sup> The [US Geological Survey](23814944.md) lists the highest elevation as 980 feet (300 m) but does not give a location.<sup>[\[197\]](#cite_note-usgs_elevations-211)</sup> Geography professor John Tichy said the highest point is the site of Waite Park Elementary School at approximately 985 feet (300 m) above sea level.<sup>[\[199\]](#cite_note-213)</sup> All of the cited sources that list locations say the highest point is within the [Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast,_Minneapolis) section of the city.

By 1977, when the most were lost to the epidemic and the city began its control program, the Twin Cities had lost 192,000 elm trees to the disease, and more than 30,000 diseased trees were found in Minneapolis.<sup>[\[440\]](#cite_note-459)</sup>

Retrieved July 24, 2024. For a few years in the mid-'80s, not long after Athens and sometime before Seattle, the epicenter of American underground rock was Minneapolis....But genius can put any town on the map, which Prince accomplished for his home city with 1984 album and film Purple Rain, whose prominent concert footage was shot at a local club called First Avenue.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorriganSigelman2018x"At_the_dawn_of_the_1980s,_the_Twin_Cities_music_scene_was_poised_to_explode...._Husker_Du,_the_Replacements,_Loud_Fast_Rules_(later_Soul_Asylum)_and_others_were_fast_building_rabid_local_followings_and_would_soon_achieve_national_acclaim...._At_the_same_time,_a_vibrant_R&B,_funk,_and_soul_scene_was_maturing_and_forming_what_would_be_known_as_the_"Minneapolis_sound"._The_young_guitar_and_songwriting_virtuoso_Prince_was_perfecting_his_innovative_and_infectious_style..."-385]: &#x20;[Corrigan & Sigelman 2018](#CITEREFCorriganSigelman2018), p. x, "At the dawn of the 1980s, the Twin Cities music scene was poised to explode.... Husker Du, the Replacements, Loud Fast Rules (later Soul Asylum) and others were fast building rabid local followings and would soon achieve national acclaim.... At the same time, a vibrant R\&B, funk, and soul scene was maturing and forming what would be known as the "Minneapolis sound". The young guitar and songwriting virtuoso Prince was perfecting his innovative and infectious style...".

[^cite_note-386]: &#x20;Matos, Michaelangelo (March 14, 2016). ["Everybody Is a Star: How the Rock Club First Avenue Made Minneapolis the Center of Music in the '80s"](https://pitchfork.com/features/article/9832-everybody-is-a-star-how-the-rock-club-first-avenue-made-minneapolis-the-center-of-music-in-the-80s/). *[Pitchfork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitchfork_\(website\))*. [Condé Nast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cond%C3%A9_Nast). Retrieved April 16, 2023. Minneapolis music peaked in the middle of 1984: Purple Rain in theaters, the release of Hüsker Dü's Zen Arcade, and the 12" of the Replacements' "I Will Dare". By 1987, that crazy peak had subsided. Hüsker Dü released another double LP in January...but broke up shortly after their manager David Savoy's suicide. On May 27, the Replacements played First Avenue for the last time. And in September, Prince opened Paisley Park Studios way out in Chanhassen....
